How to record future retcons

For each change, record the affected concept, editor tag, previous wording or rule, new wording or rule, status (proposed or established), and a link to the updated section. Do not attribute an original concept to a later editor merely because they revised it.

EXSLACURRENT

Current established canon — latest decisions

Canon additions — Spiritus, contracts, and the Wyrm [EX][SLA]

Editorial status: Additive updates from the November 2026 lore discussion. Existing definitions and historical source text are preserved. Established clarifications are separated from developing hypotheses.

Established clarification: Sovereignty and Sanctum

Additional Clarification: Higher Sovereignty makes a person's Sanctum more difficult to lower through manipulation, deception, or coercion. Even when the Sanctum is lowered, elevated Sovereignty continues to resist Siphoning.

This does not make the Sanctum impossible to lower voluntarily. Spiritus retains its exact definition: “The innate ability to strengthen one’s own Sovereignty.” Spiritus does not directly strengthen Sanctum. A Steward generally must induce a lowered Sanctum to gain access for Siphoning; strong Sovereignty makes that manipulation harder and continues resisting extraction afterward.

Established clarification: nonphysical Binding Contracts

A Binding Contract attaches to a soul through authorized consent and access to Sovereignty. Interaction with a living, physical person does not make the resulting soul contract a physical manifestation. The Wyrm can maintain many nonphysical Binding Contracts simultaneously while remaining limited to one physical manifestation at a time. The Codex is the current physical manifestation. Authorized access is not a forced breach of Sanctum, nor does a contract grant unlimited control over the signatory.

Established distinction: Transmogrification and manifestation

Steward Soulcraft can Transmogrify an existing soul into a physical object. This is distinct from the Wyrm manifesting himself physically as the Codex. Neither process establishes that souls are inherently physical; their underlying nature remains unresolved.

Established distinction: prediction versus control

The Wyrm can analyze possible futures and make influential choices, but cannot guarantee that a predicted outcome will occur. He can introduce knowledge, opportunities, contracts, or a permitted manifestation; subsequent events remain beyond his direct control. The Stewards, by contrast, deliberately manipulate people, civilizations, and political circumstances to engineer preferred outcomes. Neither party is guaranteed success. The Wyrm's interventions can influence reality without amounting to direct control over the resulting history.

Developing lore: why the Wyrm introduced Spiritus [EX][SLA] — proposed, not yet confirmed

The Wyrm may have taught Spiritus to an early Ancient King after predicting that greater Sovereignty could support stronger voluntary Binding Contracts. He could foresee this as a possibility but could not confirm it without observing a civilization cultivate Spiritus over generations. If confirmed, the Kingdom's development—and potentially the Bloodline Testament—would provide the real-world result of that experiment. Stronger contractual capacity would belong to the consenting soul; it would not grant the Wyrm arbitrary new powers.

Developing lore: the Stewards' missed opportunity [EX][SLA] — hypothesis, not yet confirmed

Highly developed Sovereignty made the Ancient Kingdom's souls difficult to access and Siphon. Yet, if such souls could be exploited, they might yield greater sustenance or enable stronger, more stable, or more complex Transmogrified constructs. The Stewards' destruction of the Kingdom could therefore have eliminated an exceptionally valuable resource while removing a perceived threat. These possible benefits are untested, not established Soulcraft mechanics. The Stewards' ultimate endgame and their first discovery of the Kingdom's resistance remain open for PDN's development.

Continuity boundaries

The Wyrm's original purpose, what “the game” means, the nature of souls, the precise limits of Transmogrified consciousness, and whether the Wyrm anticipated the Codex catastrophe remain unresolved. This update does not establish the cause of Oathbreaker's lost contract memory or how his Codex contract persisted through death and Restoration. No new motive or ultimate objective is assigned to the Queen or the Stewards.

Soul principles

Sanctum protects a soul's Sovereignty (its authority over itself). Spiritus is the innate ability to strengthen one's own Sovereignty. Strong Spiritus increases resistance to Siphoning and manipulation, including when Sanctum is lowered, but is not immunity and does not strengthen the Sanctum itself.

Inscripture incorporates a soul's intact consciousness into the Library for the Wyrm's knowledge collection. Inscribed souls retain identity, memories, consciousness, Sanctum and Sovereignty. They remain conscious, can interact with other souls in their respective collections, and can refuse requests. They cannot leave their collections under their own power or be Restored. Their experience resembles a shared afterlife, though their confinement remains real. No forced dormancy is canon.

Preservation is placed by the Queen on a living being and takes precedence over Inscripture at death; it allows Restoration. Preservation and Inscripture are different powers, not a strength ranking.

Soulcraft is the Stewards' innate capacity to extract, manipulate, reshape and consume souls. Siphoning extracts spiritual essence; Transmogrification can turn souls into physical objects; Consumption uses souls as sustenance, with complete Consumption permanently destroying a soul. Exact mechanics and limits of partial Consumption and Transmogrified consciousness remain open. Elite Stewards may forcibly breach Sanctums; ordinary Stewards rely on lowered Sanctums.

The natural fate of souls not Inscribed, Preserved, or Transmogrified is unknown. Consumption is a distinct destructive outcome.

The Wyrm and its bargains

The Wyrm is an Ancient Anomaly with a vast Library of knowledge. It occasionally offers civilizations, groups or bloodlines a continuing communal afterlife through Inscripture in exchange for the knowledge of their members. It acquires knowledge passively; not all knowledge is equally valuable, and it does not pursue only exceptional knowledge. It is indifferent to a civilization's survival. The Wyrm's deeper motives and the game remain unexplained.

The Wyrm introduced Spiritus to an early Ancient King from the Library's many possible offerings. Its accumulated knowledge allows it to analyze those it meets and anticipate desires and outcomes; the precise reason Spiritus was chosen remains unknown. The Kingdom cultivated Spiritus for generations and developed a largely peaceful, contemplative, monk-like culture with strong Sovereignty.

The Bloodline Testament is the Ancient King's agreement with the Wyrm, pledging his descendants to Inscripture upon death. It is an agreement, not a mortal spell compelling the Wyrm. The abandoned idea that every descendant must personally pair Spiritus with the Wyrm to finalize it is not canon.

The Library does not reveal what it is. It reveals what each visitor is capable of perceiving. Books and pages are the Oathbreaker's subjective perception; souls are not literally turned into pages. Only the Stewards' race has the established capacity to make physical objects out of souls.

Historical arc and Accord

Rift Convergence begins → Ancient Kingdom is destroyed → Accord is established.

The Stewards detected the Ancient Kingdom's Spiritus as a threat to their soul-exploitation system. They did not manipulate or redirect Rifts themselves. Instead, they manipulated the kingdoms and civilizations entering through the Rifts, exploited the chaos of first contact, falsely blamed the Ancient Kingdom, and directed the resulting hostility toward it. The Stewards orchestrated the Kingdom's destruction through these civilizations rather than attacking it directly. The first Rift members subsequently founded the Accord upon the Kingdom's ruins. The precise identities of participating civilizations and their individual grievances remain to be developed, as do the Accord's exact founding actors and whether they knew of the Stewards' deception. The Stewards were unaware of the Wyrm. The Wyrm did not care that the Kingdom fell.

The broader Core Source premise remains: the Accord later facilitated interworld exchange; its leaders were compromised by Stewards, who engineered war and dependency. The Queen's soulless insectoid colony and the Champions remain important.

Oathbreaker origin

The Oathbreaker's father incurred a crushing debt to an influential broker, who held the knight's hometown hostage. The exact confrontation remains open.

The Queen sent her biologically connected ants through Rifts and saw the Oathbreaker through one of her ants. She selected him for reasons belonging to her own game; her motive remains private and unexplained. She is not omniscient or omnipotent: her distant perception depends on her insects. The date of this observation is not fixed. She placed Preservation on the Oathbreaker while he was alive, before their first meeting. The exact method by which she applied Preservation at a distance is not yet defined. During its ancient slumber, the Wyrm sensed the Queen's influence subverting the game and manifested the Codex. Its manifestation caused a town-sized outward explosion followed by violent inward collapse. The Oathbreaker died in the catastrophe, but the Queen Restored him; he met the Queen after Restoration.

The townspeople died and were not Inscribed into the Library or Ancestral Domain. Their souls' fate is unknown. The Oathbreaker's broken oath is his failure to protect them, not intentional wrongdoing or reckless use of the Codex.

The Oathbreaker unknowingly descends from an illegitimate surviving branch of the Ancient Kingdom's royal line. His Codex is a manifestation of the Wyrm and conduit to the Ancestral Domain, not a container for souls. Through Domain Invocation, he requests a voluntary Ancestral Blessing; Inscribed ancestors retain Sovereignty and may refuse. The Codex is a signature weapon outside the normal deck.

Reserved narrative mysteries

The game; the Wyrm's ultimate purpose; the Queen's motive for Preserving Oathbreaker; the Library's objective form; and the natural fate of ordinary souls remain intentionally unknown.

EDITORIAL

Plot-hole and continuity audit

Type Issue Current handling
Open How did the royal bloodline survive the Kingdom's destruction and lose knowledge of its origins? Survival and Oathbreaker's descent are established; chain of descent is not.
Open What exactly happened during the father's debt crisis immediately before the Codex manifested? Human-scale event sequence remains to be written.
Open How does the Wyrm acquire a soul's knowledge while leaving Sanctum and Sovereignty intact? Result is established; access mechanism is undefined.
Open How does a pact by the King apply to descendants not yet born without compromising Sovereignty? Bloodline Testament remains established; precise metaphysics need care.
Open Do Transmogrified souls retain consciousness and Sanctum? Can Siphoning gradually consume a soul? Neither mechanic finalized.
Open — memory gap Why does the Oathbreaker not remember making his Binding Contract with the Wyrm? The contract, and that it preceded the Codex's first manifestation, are established; the cause of the lost memory is not.
Undefined mechanic How did the Oathbreaker's Codex contract persist through his death and Restoration? Death, Restoration and the continuing contract are established; how the contract carried over is not.
Undefined mechanic What is the relationship between the Library and the Ancestral Domain? Inscripture places souls in the Library, and the Oathbreaker's Inscribed ancestors are reached through the Ancestral Domain; whether the Domain is part of the Library or separate is not defined.
Open — competing claims Does the Bloodline Testament keep any claim on the Oathbreaker's soul now that he is Preserved? Preservation takes precedence over Inscripture at death; whether the Testament's pledge lapses, waits, or still has any effect is not defined.
Plot-hole risk If elite Stewards can forcibly breach Sanctums, why did they destroy the Kingdom through other civilizations instead of attacking it themselves? The indirect destruction is established; the Stewards' reason for choosing it is not.
Continuity risk How do the Stewards remain unaware of the Wyrm after the Codex's town-sized catastrophe? The Stewards' ignorance of the Wyrm is established; whether they noticed the catastrophe, and why it did not lead them to the Wyrm, is not.
Intentional mystery What is the game, and why does the Queen's Preservation subvert it? Do not prematurely explain.
Intentional mystery Where do unclaimed souls go after death? Unknown by design.
CSORIGINAL SOURCE — UNALTERED

Source: story (1).md. The text below is reproduced from the provided original story, including its own proposal and open-question labels. It may conflict with newer canon above.

Story

The overarching story and how it fits the game. It has three kinds of content:

  • Decided: everything not marked otherwise.
  • Proposal: an idea that hasn't been agreed yet.
  • Open questions: listed at the end.

Names are working names. The alternatives are listed under Names.

Premise

Decades ago, Rifts began opening between worlds. The worlds formed the Accord and peacefully shared technology, magic, resources and people. Years later, tension grew between them and became an all-out war.

The war was engineered. One of the Accord's founding worlds is ruled by the Stewards, a small group who have secretly controlled every Accord leader. Their aim is to make life so unbearable that the other worlds willingly come under their rule.

Then a new world was discovered: a primitive, insect-like colony that the Stewards have never touched. The Accord calls a truce to fight it. Heroes from the warring worlds meet on that front and are "saved" by the colony's queen, who sends them to take on the Stewards.

Souls

  • Sanctum: every soul has a sanctum, a barrier around it.

  • Sovereignty: a soul's rule over itself, which its sanctum guards. Anyone who tampers with a soul is taking some of its sovereignty.

  • Ruling: to rule another soul's sovereignty, your soul has to be stronger than theirs.

  • Siphoning: any soul can draw strength from another, but only both ways at once:

    • To receive, a soul has to lower its own sanctum.
    • A lowered sanctum lets the other soul draw from it too.

    So siphoning is always an exchange.

  • It doesn't scale: it's easy for all of a king's subjects to trust the king, but the king can't open himself to each of them in the same way. Ordinary siphoning stays between a few close souls.

  • The Stewards' race: only their race can produce a soul born without a sanctum. No other race can.

The Stewards

Stripped of their soul's sanctum, the Stewards must siphon other souls to survive, and every soul they take makes their own sovereignty harder to seize.

The Stewards are a few individuals who rule their own world. They're the same race as its people, but each was born without a sanctum.

They have a full soul with no barrier around it, so they can draw from others without opening anything in return. Their siphoning is one-way, and its scale has no limit. When other souls attach to theirs, their own personality doesn't change: they take strength, not identity.

  • Stewards: what they're publicly called.
  • Siphons: what they do.
  • The Stripped: what they are.

The Skein

The Stewards' followers are the Skein, and one follower is "one of the Skein". In public, a skein is a gathered whole, like a flock of geese or swans in flight. It's also a coil of spun wool, which hints at their fleece being spun up and taken.

Followers rise through four ranks, one for each stage of lowering their sanctum:

Rank Sanctum Meaning
Sheaf Raised Newly gathered in, still whole
Shorn Lowered Gave their fleece to the Stewards
Sheltered Mostly lowered Taken into the Stewards' care, an honoured inner circle
Stilled Fully lowered Fully at peace, with no colour of their own left

Each rank sounds like an honour in public. In truth, being Sheltered means being next in line to be consumed.

Rules of their power

  1. Force: they can force their way through a raised sanctum, but only for a few people. Those few are all the leaders of the Accord.
  2. Control: the less a soul lowers its sanctum to them, the less they can control it.
  3. Scale: they can control any number of souls whose sanctums are lowered enough.
  4. Choice: they can't force anyone to lower their sanctum.
  5. Transformation: a sanctum lowered far enough lets them modify the soul itself, which transforms the body.

Why no one can rule them

A Steward's sovereignty has no sanctum to guard it, so only the strength of their soul protects it.

  • When they're weak: a newborn Steward, or one who has grown weak, can be ruled by anyone with a stronger soul.
  • Once they're established: so many souls are attached to them that their soul's strength is unmatched, and no one is strong enough to rule them.

What they gain

  • Siphoned strength: what they draw through lowered sanctums makes their soul stronger and their body healthier and stronger. That's why they can't be assassinated, and why no one can rule them.
  • Souls give lifespan: the Stewards consume the souls of their followers to stay alive. A consumed soul is gone, and a person without a soul can't become a follower.

Why they need the war

The Stewards consume souls faster than their own world can replace them through births. Their world can't sustain them, so they need followers from other worlds.

Force can't get them there, because of rules 1 and 4. They use the leaders they control to drive the worlds to war, so that people choose the Stewards for themselves.

Their world

From outside it looks like a utopia. There's no conflict, and the Stewards have no reason to abuse their people because they already have nearly everything they want.

The bodies of the consumed decompose in a way that makes a perfect building material, so every building on the Stewards' world is made from dead followers.

Keeping the secret

The Stewards control what their followers and the Accord's leaders say, so information is easy to manipulate. Assassination attempts don't need a real cover-up: the Stewards only have to say security stopped them.

The new world

The new world is a primitive, ant-colony-like civilization of insect-like creatures. The AI world is a different world.

  • The subjects: they have no souls, so the Stewards have no hold on them. The queen sends them out to events.
  • The queen: she has a soul, but has never come into direct contact with the Stewards.
  • Consuming: she can consume someone and then give birth to them again, and she holds the souls she eats.
  • Memories: she keeps the memories of the souls she eats.
  • Her limit: she can only keep a certain number of souls and memories. That's why she isn't a dictator or a conqueror, and why she looks for talented people.
  • Sensing: she can notice the souls of the Stewards' followers being consumed, in real time.
  • The truce: humans at least have an aversion to insects, which helps make a truce against the colony believable.
  • Advancing: she tries to advance her own civilization, and players' unlocks, updates and achievements show up on her world.

The Errants (The Heros / Player characters)

The Errants aren't consumables. Their souls are linked together, and that link is what lets them fight on:

  • Staying together: while they're close together, they can recover from near death.
  • Going down: being near death weakens the connection.
  • All down: their bodies lose connection to the queen, and they respawn at her world.
  • A wipe ends the run: the errants return to the queen's world, and that rebirth is the story's reason for the run loop.

Gods

Gods embody concepts. They gain power through their concept and through offerings.

An offering is given outward, and the giver never lowers their sanctum to make it. Gods therefore never siphon and have nothing to do with sanctums, which keeps them clearly separate from the Stewards.

Who works with souls

Who How What they can't do
The Stewards Draw one-way from souls whose sanctums are lowered, and consume souls Survive without consuming
Everyone else Share strength two-way with a few close souls Take without lowering their own sanctum
The queen Consumes body and soul whole, and holds souls and memories Hold more than her limit
The errants Their souls are linked to each other through the queen Take anything one-way
Gods Gain power from their concept and from offerings Siphon

Worlds

World Idea Status
The Stewards' world The false utopia built from its dead Decided
The new world The queen's colony, which advances over time Decided
Magic world Typical fantasy Idea
AI world Futuristic, full of sentient robots and AI Idea
Medieval world Gods, who embody concepts, and spirits Idea

Visual direction

Across the whole game

  • Models carry their own colours: team colour isn't painted onto a model, and neither is affinity colour.
  • Team colour is an outline: each unit is outlined in its team's colour, so it reads against any world's palette.
  • Affinity colours stay on cards and the interface.
  • Few materials: most of a model is flat colour, with a material only where it earns its place.

The two central worlds

The Stewards' world should look beautiful and feel wrong, while the queen's colony should look frightening and feel honest. Players should be drawn to the utopia and put off by the hive at first, then feel the reverse once they learn the truth.

The Stewards' race

They're sheep–deer people with swan-like features: about as docile as a creature can look, which makes the Stewards a literal wolf in sheep's clothing. Swans add beauty and, in English at least, a sense of love.

  • Build: upright and slender, with a deer-like frame, hooves, large dark eyes and soft ears. It stays on a humanoid rig, with the ears, tail and horns as extra bones.
  • Fleece: modelled as chunky, sculpted clumps rather than realistic fur.
  • Horns: curled ram horns that grow in rings.
  • Horn rings are limited: a Steward has millions of souls attached, so the rings can't count souls. There are only ever a few.
  • The Stewards are born without fleece: their bodies lack fleece just as their souls lack a sanctum, which makes "the Stripped" a literal name as well.
  • Mantles of their followers' wool: each Steward wears a mantle made from the Skein's wool, and it covers their whole body. Their bare bodies are a reveal, saved for key story moments.
  • Only the Stewards wear cloaks: a cloak alone marks a Steward, even at a distance on the board.
  • Transforming drains colour: as a follower lowers their sanctum and is transformed, they lose their colour. This shows their sovereignty and individuality being taken away.

Swan features, without wings

The race is swan-like through its neck, markings and posture, and has no wings.

  • The neck: long, with a gentle S-shaped curve and an upright posture, like a swan on water. This is the strongest swan signal.
  • Face markings: a dark mask across the bridge of the nose and a small dark knob above the muzzle, like a mute swan's, on a sheep's face.
  • Feathers at the neck: wool on the body gives way to short, smooth feathers up the neck and around the head.
  • Mantles shaped like folded wings: the Stewards' mantles drape from the shoulders like folded wings. The only wings they have are clothing.
  • Movement: smooth and gliding, with the head held still while walking.

What the Skein wear

The Skein are mostly natural: their own fleece is their clothing. Each rank adds one small item, and the items change from personal to livestock-like until nothing is left.

  • Why no cloaks: a cloak would hide their fleece, and with it the colour loss that shows their sovereignty being taken. Natural fleece also keeps the saying clear: followers wear their own wool, while Stewards wear other people's.
  • Being Shorn never means being bare: shearing takes only part of the fleece, such as the shoulders or a ceremonial lock, and it grows back short. Bareness belongs to the Stewards alone.
Sanctum Rank Look Item
Raised Sheaf Full fleece in their own colours and markings, and an individual face A personal token, such as a woven sash, beads or a braided ribbon
Lowered Shorn Fleece cropped short where it was cut, colour fading, expressions softening A plain band marking the shearing
Mostly lowered Sheltered Pale and nearly identical, with calm, mask-like faces A thin band around the neck, or a small bell
Fully lowered Stilled No colour of their own left Nothing: no possessions, nothing of their own

The bell is an honour in public, a mark of being close to the Stewards. Privately, it's a livestock bell.

A concept sheet exists, made quickly with an image generator. It's a starting point, not a final design. Before it's used:

  • Mantles: they need to cover the Stewards' whole bodies, where the sheet shows bare torsos.
  • Horn rings: they should be far fewer.
  • Swan features: it doesn't have them yet.
  • Followers' cloaks: the sheet puts followers in cloaks and robes. They should be in natural fleece with rank items.
  • Labels: it calls the followers "the Fold", which is now the Skein.

How it fits the game

  • Runs: the structure is closer to Slay the Spire 2 than to Helldivers. Each run stands alone, and the story comes out across many runs.
  • The map: choosing where to go means choosing between worlds. Each world has its own terrain, generation and scenarios.
  • The queen's world: it shows what players have unlocked and achieved.
  • War news: short broadcasts give the Accord's version of events, which can be propaganda, alongside the truth.
  • The ending: left open for now.

What already exists

This is based on CLAUDE.md as of 2026-11-06.

  • Routes: a game moves through a route of stops, either generated in rows or fixed (RouteDefinition, routes.json). After a win, players vote on which exit to take next.
  • Encounters: each stop has its own map, map seed, activity and entities (EncounterDefinition, encounters.json). A fight's objectives say how it ends, and the default fight is lost when all players are down (players_downed).
  • Recovery: after a win, every downed unit a player controls gets back up (ReviveRules), and cards can revive units (ReviveEffect).
  • Available as data: maps, biomes, map features, scenarios, and pools filtered by affinity.
  • Available as rules: downed units, transforms, statuses, and unlocks shared across co-op players.
  • Staged, not yet in CLAUDE.md: a campaign of acts, each with its own route, is staged in changes 0127 to 0130. An act is the natural place for a world.
  • Missing:
    • A world definition that groups encounters, map generation and an affinity.
    • Recovery during a fight from linked souls staying close together.
    • The queen's world as a hub between runs.

Proposals

None of these are agreed yet.

Souls and the Stewards

  • Consumption is complete siphoning: consuming a soul means siphoning it until nothing is left.

  • Rule 1 needs contact: forced control only works through direct contact. If the Stewards reached the queen, they'd gain her subjects and every soul she holds, so her location has to stay secret.

  • Force uses the ruling rule: forcing through a raised sanctum works because a Steward's soul is stronger than the target's.

  • The heroes share two-way: their linked souls use ordinary two-way siphoning. That's why staying close lets them recover, and it's what the Stewards can never have.

  • Opening is a risk: a hero who betrays the others, or is taken by the Stewards, could do real harm because they're open to each other.

  • Young Stewards are ruled: a newborn Steward is weak, so the established Stewards rule them from birth. That keeps the group small and loyal, and stops rivals from growing.

  • A way to beat them: every sanctum raised against a Steward cuts off a soul and weakens them. Once a Steward's soul is weaker than someone else's, their sovereignty can be ruled. This makes "weaken, then rule or break" the path to taking one down.

  • Linked heroes add up: the heroes' combined strength could be what finally outmatches a weakened Steward.

  • Clearer sanctum levels:

    | How far the sanctum is lowered | What the Stewards can do | |---|---| | Not at all | Forced control, limited to a few people | | Partly | Nudge moods and beliefs | | Mostly | Directly command | | Fully | Reshape the soul and body |

Story

  • The truce is the first clue: every leader agrees within days after years of war. The Stewards also broker it and take the credit, so more people lower their sanctums to them.
  • The truce is well timed: war deaths waste souls the Stewards could have had, so they want it to end at the right moment.
  • The propaganda is true: the Accord says the queen eats people, and she does. The heroes learn that she ate them too.
  • Why the heroes matter: their souls are held by the queen, so the Stewards can't take or control them, while armies can be turned.
  • How she learned the truth: she consumed someone who knew, such as a puppet or a Steward's servant, and nobody believes her.
  • Her progress has a cost: each advance comes from the memories of someone she ate, so a forge might appear because she consumed a blacksmith.
  • Her progress stays: what she learns passes permanently to her brood, so the hub never goes backwards when she releases a soul.
  • The god of war: the war has made the god of war very powerful, so it has a reason to want the fighting to go on.

The Stewards' world

  • "Joining the city": the citizens know what the buildings are made of and treat it as an honour.
  • Empty districts: the population falls while the city grows, so there are whole districts of perfect, empty homes that visitors never see.
  • Exported stone: the Accord buys the material as a luxury stone, so the dead are in every world.
  • A trail of the missing: assassins sent by other worlds vanish, and players can find the records.
  • Mating for life: like swans, the Skein form lifelong pairs who share two-way. That love is exactly what the Stewards can never have.
  • "Stock": what the Stewards call their people in private. It sounds like "good stock" but means livestock.

Visuals

  • What the horn rings mark: something with only a few steps, such as age or rank. A Steward's horns could be so finely ringed that they look polished smooth, reading as "beyond counting" without showing a number.

  • The shearing festival: followers give their fleece to the Stewards in a festival, the public version of lowering a sanctum. It marks the step from Sheaf to Shorn.

  • The bell's sound: a quiet chime when Sheltered followers are nearby, both as atmosphere and as a cue for players.

  • The bare Steward is a black swan: without the mantle, a Steward looks lean and sharp, a little wolfish, while still clearly the same race. Their bare body is dark, like a black swan, which also means something rare and unexpected. The pale mantle against the dark body makes the reveal clear on screen.

  • The ugly duckling, reversed: young swans are grey and become pure white as adults. The Skein do the same as they're transformed, and their world calls it becoming beautiful, when in truth it's losing themselves.

  • Beautiful but dangerous: swans are fiercely territorial, so the race's elegance can have an edge underneath.

  • The final stage foreshadows the city: at the last stage, a follower's colourless wool and horn look like the ivory the city is built from.

  • Not too cute: eyes a little too still and smiles a little too wide, so players sense something is off.

  • The Stewards look like caretakers: plain robes, aprons, and tools such as crooks and pruning shears. They never wear crowns.

  • Telling worlds apart: each world should be recognisable by shape and contrast alone.

    | World | Shapes | Materials | Palette | |---|---|---|---| | The Stewards' world | Smooth curves, symmetry, buildings that look grown | Ivory and nacre, from the dead | Soft white, pale gold | | The queen's colony | Irregular tunnels and chambers | Mud, wax, resin, chitin | Dark amber, warm glowing greens | | Magic world | Spirals, floating stone, towers | Crystal, polished stone, runes | Jewel tones | | AI world | Hard angles, grids, modules | Dark metal, glass, lines of light | Graphite, with cyan and orange | | Gods world | Heavy blocks, tall pillars, banners | Weathered stone, wood, cloth | Earth tones, firelight |

  • Keep the Stewards' world and the AI world apart: the Stewards' world is soft, warm and organic, while the AI world is hard, dark and angular.

  • The queen's world shows what she ate: she starts with mud and wax, then builds copies of things from eaten memories, such as a forge in resin. Each copy is slightly wrong.

  • Showing souls:

    | Idea | How it could look | |---|---| | Sanctum | A steady ring of light in a neutral colour | | Lowered sanctum | The ring dims and opens | | Two-way siphoning | Threads braided between two souls, flowing both ways | | The Stewards siphoning | One-way streams pulling toward someone with no ring | | The heroes' link | Threads between nearby heroes that thin as they go down, which can also show recovery range in a fight | | Rifts | Tears showing another world's sky, kept different from soul effects |

Names

Thing Name Alternatives
A soul's barrier Sanctum Bastion
What a sanctum guards Sovereignty
The villains, publicly The Stewards Trustees, Custodians, Tenders, Caretakers
The villains, by what they do Siphons Wells, Tithers, Conduits
The villains, by what they are The Stripped The Bare, the Open
The villains' followers The Skein The Staple, the Sheaf, the Swathe, the Fold
Ranks of followers Sheaf, Shorn, Sheltered, Stilled Sworn for the first rank
The villains' race Not yet named
Opening up to another soul Kinship (proposal)
The tears between worlds Rifts Breaches, Seams, Crossings
The alliance The Accord

Stewards, Siphons, Skein, Sanctum and Sovereignty all start with S, and Skein even sounds a little like "sheep", but only in English. Translators should pick each word by its meaning, not its sound.

Open questions

Story

  • What is the Stewards' race called, and how many Stewards are there?
  • What are the two timeline dates? One suggestion is about 60 years since the Rifts and 12 since the war began.
  • What do the Stewards' world and the queen's world call themselves?
  • What makes one soul stronger than another? Is it only the souls attached to it, or do age, will or training count too?
  • Can one Steward rule another, and is there a strongest Steward?
  • Do the souls the queen holds add to her strength?
  • Does a sanctum rise again once it's been lowered?
  • Does siphoning damage a sanctum for good?
  • What happens to a person after their soul is consumed: do they die at once, or live on for a while?
  • Do followers know that consumption is happening?
  • Can the queen release a soul to make room for another, and what happens to it?
  • Why does the queen care about the war, beyond survival?
  • How does the queen talk to the heroes?
  • Do the heroes ever get their souls back, and does she gain anything from holding them?
  • Do AIs have souls, and do they have sanctums? If they don't, the Stewards have no hold on the AI world either, and the queen's world is less unique.
  • Who are the heroes, and which worlds do they come from?
  • How does the story end? Can the Stewards be killed at all?

Visuals

  • What do the horn rings mark, if not souls?
  • Can followers be told apart from each other at each stage, and how much of that needs to show on the hex board?
  • What do the queen and her subjects look like?

Gameplay

  • What counts as "close together" for linked souls, in hexes, including for multi-tile units?
  • How much does each downed hero weaken the link, and how?
  • How do linked souls work for a solo player?
  • Does each world get its own affinity? This affects how cards are designed from now on.
  • What carries over between runs, and how much of the story depends on it?
  • Do the worlds keep a fixed role in a run, or can they appear at any stage?
EXHISTORICAL VERSION 2.3

Source: The_Rift_Convergence_Story_Sanctum_and_Sovereignty_v2_3.md. Preserved as a historical snapshot, not a replacement for newer canon. Editor attribution remains unassigned pending confirmation; [EX][PDN] is reserved for the other editor and [EX][SLA] for your contributions.

THE RIFT CONVERGENCE

Story — Sanctum & Sovereignty Edition

Version: 2.3
Status: Consolidated story and Oathbreaker retcon
Terminology: The Stewards replace X / the Coalition. Faith is removed from this story's supernatural system.

Canon priority: The latest decisions about the Stewards, Sanctum, Sovereignty, Will, and the Oathbreaker supersede older Faith-based explanations. [Established] indicates specified canon; [Open] indicates mechanics or events not yet settled.

I. Core Story

Decades ago, Rifts began opening unpredictably between different worlds. These pathways connected civilizations that had previously been isolated, without any single world serving as the center of the network. The connected civilizations formed the Accord, initially sharing technology, magic, resources, and people. Tensions eventually escalated into a devastating interworld war.

The war was engineered. One of the Accord's founding worlds is ruled by the Stewards, a small group who secretly control the Accord's leaders. Their goal is to make other worlds desperate enough to willingly accept their rule and lower their souls' defenses, allowing the Stewards to siphon strength and sustain themselves by consuming souls.

A new Rift reveals the Queen's primitive, insectoid colony. Its soulless subjects resist the Stewards' ordinary soul-based influence. The Accord calls a truce against the colony; heroes from formerly hostile worlds meet on that front, and the Queen selects Champions to oppose the Stewards for reasons of her own.

Core factions

Faction / place Role
The Accord Interworld alliance, compromised through leaders controlled by the Stewards.
The Stewards Rulers who use asymmetrical Siphoning and soul Consumption to sustain power and lifespan.
The Queen's colony Soulless insectoid society; her scouts extend her perception across the world.
Champions Soul-bearing individuals selected by the Queen for Preservation and eventual Restoration.
Rifts Unpredictably appearing pathways connecting different worlds; no universal hub world.

II. Cosmic Principles — Souls, Sanctum, and Sovereignty

Term Definition
Soul An individual's spiritual identity and consciousness.
Sanctum The protective barrier surrounding a soul.
Sovereignty A soul's authority over itself, protected by its Sanctum.
Will Inward conviction and disciplined spiritual exertion; the Oathbreaker amplifies his determination with it to pass through an eligible retained soul's intact Sanctum.
Siphoning Drawing strength through a connection between souls. For ordinary souls, the exchange is reciprocal.
Consumption Permanent destruction of a soul for the Stewards' continued lifespan.
Retention The Archivist's keeping of real, conscious souls without their capacity for bodily restoration.
Preservation The Queen's protection of a living soul's capacity for later restoration.
Restoration The Queen's return of an already-preserved soul to embodied life.

Sanctum rules [Established]

  • Every ordinary soul has a Sanctum that protects its Sovereignty.
  • To receive strength through ordinary Siphoning, a soul must lower its own Sanctum, exposing itself to reciprocal exchange.
  • Ordinary Siphoning is mutual and generally limited to a small number of close souls.
  • A lowered Sanctum can expose its owner to manipulation, loss of Sovereignty, and—if lowered sufficiently—alteration of the soul and body.
  • No individual can simply force someone to choose to lower their Sanctum.
  • Faith is not part of this cosmology. Earlier descriptions of Faith as the universal power or of Will as inward Faith are retired.

Will and Sovereignty [Established]

Will amplifies the Oathbreaker's determination, allowing his spiritual influence to pass through an eligible retained soul's Sanctum without breaking, lowering, weakening, or damaging it. Through Ancestral Dominion, he can then harness that soul's Sovereignty to invoke its skills and abilities using The Codex.

The Sanctum remains intact, and the retained soul remains a conscious individual with its own identity and will. Harnessing Sovereignty does not mean owning or permanently controlling the soul: an invocation may be compelled, but it does not create loyalty, consent, or forgiveness.

This is not Siphoning. The Oathbreaker does not require the soul to lower its Sanctum, does not enter a reciprocal strength exchange, and does not use the Stewards' methods of exploitation or forced breach.

[Open] How much determination is required for passage, how retained souls resist invocation, and how long access to their Sovereignty lasts remain to be defined. The Sovereign Veil's bloodline access limits still apply.

III. The Stewards — The Stripped

The Stewards (also known as the Covenant) are a small group from one founding world. They belong to the same race as their subjects but are born with complete souls without Sanctums; only their race can produce such individuals.

Aspect Rule
Innate difference Born without a Sanctum; also called the Stripped.
Siphoning Can draw from souls that lower their Sanctums without exposing themselves in return.
Force Can forcibly breach raised Sanctums, but only on a small scale; the Accord's leaders are among their controlled targets.
Control More freely lowered Sanctums allow greater influence over many followers.
Transformation Sufficiently lowered Sanctums permit changes to souls and, consequently, bodies.
Consumption Permanently destroys followers' souls to extend the Stewards' lives.
Political method Manufacture war and desperation to persuade populations to accept their rule voluntarily.
Homeworld An apparent utopia whose buildings are constructed from the remains of consumed followers.

The Stewards' central vulnerability is consent at scale: they cannot compel entire civilizations to lower their Sanctums, so they engineer circumstances in which people do so willingly. Their ability to forcibly breach a few raised Sanctums does not grant unlimited coercive reach.

IV. The Queen and Her Colony

The Queen is an ancient, soul-bearing insectoid entity whose innate abilities include creating soulless insectoid life, Preservation, and Restoration. Her colony functions through independent insect instincts and a biological sensory link to its creator.

Collective Sight — The Thousand Eyes

  • Passive awareness: Insects communicate primitive impressions of movement, threats, and unusual events.
  • Focused sight: The Queen can perceive through a chosen subject's senses.
  • Collective observation: Scouts survey distant regions and help identify possible Champions.
  • Limit: Scouts must physically reach a place; they do not automatically read souls or penetrate the Sovereign Veil.

Soul continuity

  • The Queen establishes Preservation while the subject is alive.
  • At death, a previously preserved soul retains the capacity for Restoration.
  • Souls already Retained by the Archivist cannot be retroactively preserved or restored.
  • Preservation does not automatically grant the Queen the subject's consent, loyalty, or Sovereignty.
  • The Queen's methods do not require physically eating subjects; older descriptions of her consuming bodies to preserve them are retired.

The Queen's motives remain opaque. Her preserved Champions can return after a party wipe, supporting the game's roguelike run structure.

V. The Ancient Kingdom — An Oathbreaker-Related History

The ancient human kingdom arose on an Earth-like planet. Long before its destruction, an anomalous encounter with the Archivist's old-man manifestation introduced its ruler to the principles of Will; the Archivist offered knowledge and direction rather than ruling the kingdom.

Over generations, the kingdom's scholars refined Will and developed the royal inheritance called Ancestral Dominion. This was an extraordinary capacity to assert authority over eligible retained souls, not a form of the Stewards' Siphoning.

The Convergence and genocide

The kingdom's planet was caught in a Rift Convergence event. One of the newly opened Rifts happened to connect it to the Stewards' world, exposing the kingdom to their civilization and, eventually, their ambitions.

The Stewards identified the kingdom's mastery of Will and soul Sovereignty as a threat to their system of control through lowered Sanctums. During the chaos, they launched a deliberate genocide against the kingdom and the people preserving its knowledge. The Sovereign Veil did not cause the attack.

[Open] The exact process by which the Stewards learned of the kingdom's techniques, and whether they initially posed as benefactors, has not been established.

The final contingency

As part of their last defense, the king and scholars imposed the Ancestral Compact, a binding spiritual provision within the Sovereign Veil. This is not the Covenant: that name refers to the antagonistic Stewards, not to the kingdom's ancestral arrangement.

The royal line was pledged to the Ancestral Domain through a Blood Tithe: descent establishes eligibility to invoke the Compact, but does not confer limitless spiritual power. The Compact provides a conditional, inherited passage through the intact Sanctum of an eligible retained soul, allowing Ancestral Dominion to harness its Sovereignty for an invocation when the heir focuses sufficient Will.

  • Limited passage: The Compact grants access, not ownership; it does not break, weaken, lower, or damage the Sanctum.
  • Resistance: A retained soul can resist a compelled invocation, requiring greater determination from the heir.
  • Restricted inheritance: Only souls authorized by the Veil and Compact are accessible; the Archivist's older Library remains outside the heir's reach.
  • Question of consent: Whether each soul voluntarily accepted these obligations, or was bound by the kingdom's emergency decree, remains [Open].

In the kingdom's last hours, the reigning king, royal family, and surviving scholars enacted the unfinished Sovereign Veil. They sought to conceal the Archivist and the Ancestral Domain from the Stewards and save the spiritual inheritance of the royal line.

  • Concealment: Hides the Archivist, Ancestral Domain, and their spiritual connections from the Stewards.
  • Retention filter: Restricts future retention to the designated royal bloodline, reducing detectable spiritual activity.
  • Inheritance filter: Restricts the royal descendant's access to the authorized ancestral inheritance, not the Archivist's older Library.
  • Emergency: The kingdom's dying souls were retained during the final contingency, including some non-royal individuals; this does not automatically grant the Oathbreaker command over them.
  • Limitations: The Veil was untested, unfinished, lacked redundancy, and had no completed reversal spell.

The Queen is also unable to perceive the hidden Domain, an unintended consequence. Even if she could see it, the souls already under Retention lack the continuity needed for Restoration.

VI. The Archivist, the Library, and The Codex

Term Meaning
The Archivist Ancient, domain-bound entity whose innate ability is Retention.
The Library Its original pre-Veil collection of real, conscious, knowledgeable souls.
Ancestral Domain The concealed inheritance associated with the destroyed kingdom and its bloodline.
Sovereign Veil Artificial overlay limiting the Archivist's future retention and concealing the Domain.
The Codex Physical manifestation of the Archivist that requires a wielder to act.
Ancestral Dominion The Oathbreaker's inherited authority to command eligible ancestral souls.
Ancestral Compact The Veil's binding spiritual provision granting eligible heirs conditional passage through retained souls' intact Sanctums.
Blood Tithe Royal descent pledged to the Ancestral Domain, establishing eligibility rather than unrestricted power.

The Archivist cannot directly conduct campaigns in the physical world or wield The Codex independently. Its old-man form influences through teaching; its sword form provides a physical conduit for an eligible wielder.

VII. The Oathbreaker — Story Synopsis

Generations after the ancient kingdom's destruction, a knight descended unknowingly from an illegitimate branch of its royal house. He swore to defend his hometown and family, devoting his life to the protection of those under his care.

During the unfolding catastrophe, the Queen selected the living knight and established a Preservation Condition on his soul. That earlier condition threatened the Veil's pending, death-triggered Retention of his royal bloodline soul. The Archivist responded by manifesting as The Codex, exposing the knight's dormant Ancestral Dominion.

Untrained and overwhelmed, the knight used his newly accessible power recklessly. His own actions caused the breach, destroying his hometown and killing his family; the precise action that triggered the catastrophe remains [Open]. He did not intend to betray his oath, but he was responsible for failing it.

His eligible relatives were retained by the Archivist. They remain conscious, and their silent acts of care—polishing his helmet, maintaining his sword, tending his gear—express familial devotion, not Faith or supernatural power transfer. Many other ancestral souls resist him or resent being compelled.

The knight became known as the Oathbreaker. He can remove his helmet, but refuses to reveal his face in another person's presence because of his shame. Through the Queen's Preservation and Restoration, he survives repeated deaths and journeys through the Rifts in pursuit of atonement.

How the Oathbreaker harnesses ancestral Sovereignty [Revised canon]

  1. Eligibility: The Blood Tithe identifies the Oathbreaker as an heir; the Sovereign Veil limits access to authorized ancestral souls.
  2. Contact: The Codex provides a conduit to an eligible retained soul.
  3. Determination: The Oathbreaker amplifies his resolve through Will.
  4. Passage: The Ancestral Compact provides a conditional passage through the soul's intact Sanctum; his amplified Will allows him to exercise it despite resistance, without breaking or lowering the protection.
  5. Sovereignty: Ancestral Dominion enables him to harness the accessible Sovereignty for a particular invocation; this does not transfer ownership of the soul.
  6. Invocation: He channels an eligible skill, technique, or ability through The Codex.
  7. Consequences: The soul remains conscious and may resent being compelled; its Sanctum and independent identity remain intact, and the Oathbreaker cannot reach unrelated souls in the Library.

The central character conflict: The Oathbreaker can compel an ancestral invocation without breaking its soul's Sanctum, but cannot make that soul forgive him. His mastery of Will may make him more capable, yet his redemption depends on what he chooses to do with that power.

VIII. Historical Sequence

Order Event
1 The Archivist retains knowledgeable souls across ancient civilizations, forming The Library.
2 An ancient human ruler encounters the Archivist; generations develop Will and Ancestral Dominion.
3 The kingdom's Earth-like planet is caught in a Rift Convergence; a Rift connects it to the Stewards' world.
4 The Stewards discover the threat posed by the kingdom's spiritual practices and launch a genocide.
5 The king, family, and scholars enact emergency Retention, the unfinished Sovereign Veil, and its Ancestral Compact with a royal Blood Tithe.
6 Generations later, a forgotten royal descendant becomes a knight and swears his oath.
7 The Queen preserves him while alive; the Archivist manifests as The Codex.
8 The knight's uncontrolled actions cause the breach, killing his family and earning him the name Oathbreaker.
9 The Oathbreaker travels through the Rifts seeking atonement and refining control over ancestral souls.

IX. Canon Boundaries and Open Questions

Fixed boundaries

  • The Stewards / the Covenant, not X or the Coalition, are the current antagonists; Ancestral Compact is the separate name for the kingdom's spiritual binding.
  • Faith is removed; explanations based on receiving belief from others no longer apply.
  • Sanctum protects Sovereignty; ordinary Siphoning is reciprocal, while the Stewards' is asymmetrical.
  • The Blood Tithe establishes royal eligibility, and the Ancestral Compact authorizes conditional passage; Will amplifies determination to exercise it through an eligible soul's intact Sanctum; Ancestral Dominion harnesses its Sovereignty for invocations without breaking its protection or permanently owning the soul.
  • The Queen preserves living souls, and does not need to eat people to do so.
  • The Archivist's Library remains intact and inaccessible to the Oathbreaker's inherited Dominion.
  • Rifts open unpredictably between worlds, without an Earth-centered hub. The Ancient Kingdom is on an Earth-like planet, and a Rift to the Stewards' world appeared there during a convergence event.
  • The Oathbreaker caused the breach; the exact triggering act remains unsettled.

Open questions

  1. What determines the determination needed to pass through an intact Sanctum, and how does a retained soul resist invocation?
  2. Does access to harnessed Sovereignty last only for one Invocation, or can it persist longer?
  3. Were souls bound by the Ancestral Compact voluntarily or by emergency royal decree, and can they voluntarily cooperate during an invocation?
  4. How did the Stewards first recognize the kingdom's Will as a threat?
  5. What exact action by the Oathbreaker caused the breach?
  6. What is the Queen's ultimate goal in selecting and restoring Champions?
  7. Can a lowered Sanctum rise again after ordinary Siphoning or the Stewards' intrusion?

One-sentence premise: In a universe where the Stewards exploit lowered Sanctums to steal strength and Sovereignty, a disgraced knight invokes an inherited Ancestral Compact, amplifying his determination through Will to pass through the intact Sanctums of his retained ancestors and harness their Sovereignty, seeking redemption for the catastrophe he himself caused.

Additional historical lore files

These additional Markdown files are included for completeness and provenance. They are not automatically current canon.

Oathbreaker_Core_Lore_Compendium_v1.md ARCHIVE

Historical source; may contain superseded terminology or proposals.

THE RIFT CONVERGENCE

Core Lore & Oathbreaker Character Bible — Consolidated v1.0

Purpose: Compact, AI-readable reference for narrative, character design, cards, and gameplay.
Source order: Existing story.md baseline first; developments from subsequent lore discussions second.
Status key: [Established] = directly specified or accepted direction; [Working] = useful connective explanation awaiting final approval; [Open] = unanswered.
Names: The Coalition is the working name for X; the Accord is the interworld alliance, not X. Archivist, Sovereign Veil, and Ancestral Dominion are working terms used consistently here.

PART I — WHAT EXISTED BEFORE

A. Original overarching story (from the earlier story file)

  • Rifts emerged decades ago. The connected civilizations formed the Accord, exchanged culture, magic, technology, and resources, and eventually descended into war.
  • X / the Coalition secretly controls key Accord leaders. It manufactures conflict so civilizations will willingly trust its promised salvation.
  • Faith makes the Coalition's members stronger and harder to kill, and gives them influence over believers. It cannot force genuine belief.
  • Soul Consumption extends X's lifespan by permanently destroying souls. Its home world's false utopia is literally built from the remains of consumed followers.
  • A newly encountered insectoid Queen rules a colony of soulless subjects. Those subjects resist the Coalition's usual soul-targeting methods.
  • The Queen takes in and rebirths heroes. Their linked souls explain near-death recovery, party wipes, and the roguelike rebirth loop.
  • The Champions travel between worlds, uncover manipulation, and struggle to protect coexistence.
  • The original Oathbreaker concept was a disgraced knight seeking atonement and insisting his oath was not intentionally abandoned.

B. Previously clarified world direction

  • Earth is an ordinary human world transformed into the crossroads of the modern Rift network; every known modern Rift leads to Earth, not directly from one foreign world to another.
  • The Champions' objective is generally to resolve threats across Rifts, not routinely close the Rifts and sever connected communities.
  • The Coalition's world is a deceptive paradise; the Queen's world is the colony and resurrection/progression hub. These are distinct from Earth, the geographic and political hub.
  • Fantasy, artificial-intelligence, and spirit-centered worlds remain possible settings, not finalized worlds.

PART II — WHAT HAS NOW BEEN ADDED OR REFINED

  1. Faith is the primary cosmic power; Will is its inward, sub-cosmic expression, not a rival energy source.
  2. Faith empowers an existing soul and its innate abilities; Will strengthens the user's own soul through genuine self-belief. Neither grants every possible power.
  3. Ancient, soul-bearing entities obey Faith too. Rifts can expose civilizations to beings who exploit Faith, devotion, and spiritual vulnerability.
  4. The Queen is an ancient, abstract life-generating and life-restoring entity, with purposes beyond the heroes' understanding. She does not create souls out of nothing.
  5. Consume, Retain, Preserve, Restore are distinct operations. Only preserved souls can be restored.
  6. The Archivist is an ancient, conscious collector of unusually knowledgeable souls; the old man and The Codex are manifestations of this same being.
  7. The Archivist taught an ancient human king about Will, but did not create Will. The kingdom studied it over generations and developed Ancestral Dominion.
  8. X destroyed the ancient kingdom during the initial chaos of the modern Rift era. Its scholars activated the unfinished Sovereign Veil.
  9. The Veil is an artificial overlay that restricts future Archivist retention to the designated royal bloodline and limits the Oathbreaker's access to the bloodline's authorized inheritance. It does not injure or erase the Archivist's native ability.
  10. The Veil was untested, unfinished, built without redundancy, and had no completed reversal spell.
  11. The Queen's preservation bond establishes a competing condition before the Oathbreaker dies, preventing his automatic ancestral retention; her selection awakens the Archivist, not the source of his inherited power.
  12. The Oathbreaker's uncontrolled use of awakened power is connected to the breach he caused, the deaths of his family, and the title Oathbreaker.
  13. His deceased relatives are among his few genuine believers. They silently polish his helmet, sharpen his sword, and care for his equipment. Very few living people he helped also give him Faith.
  14. He can remove his helmet, but never does so in another person's sight because of his guilt.
  15. His Codex is a signature-weapon slot, not an ordinary card that can vanish in a bloated late-game deck.

PART III — COSMIC ORDER & DEFINITIONS

Reading rule: The ordering below moves from cosmic principle → derived expression → ancient entities → manifestation → mortal inheritor. It is not a claim that a metaphysical law is a combatant.

1. FAITH — Primary cosmic principle [Established]

Faith is genuine belief directed by a soul toward a being, purpose, or oneself. It amplifies the recipient's soul and its existing capabilities. External Faith comes from other independent souls; it cannot be fabricated through coercion. The Coalition can manipulate circumstances to inspire devotion but cannot directly command authentic belief into existence.

Core law: Faith gives power; the nature of the recipient determines what that power can do. More believers do not create new innate abilities, and no unlimited Faith multiplier has been established.

2. WILL — Sub-cosmic offshoot of Faith [Established]

Will is Faith directed inward: sincere self-conviction, acceptance, and belief in one's own capacity. It amplifies one's own soul, but is volatile and demands discipline and efficient control.

Will is not: a second universal source; automatic resurrection; soul creation; unrestricted soul command; a method for bypassing the Veil. External Faith and Will can coexist, but they remain expressions of one cosmic principle.

3. THE QUEEN — Ancient life-generating/restoring entity [Established direction]

An abstract, insectoid matriarch whose innate power allows her to produce living subjects, preserve souls in a restorable state, and return preserved souls to embodied life. Her own soul makes her subject to Faith. Her colony's ordinary subjects are soulless, so they cannot provide soul-derived Faith or be controlled by X through its usual soul-based methods.

Scope: The Queen is a creator of living bodies and life processes, not the proven creator of souls or of all cosmic life. Her decisions serve her own game, which need not align with the Champions' interests.

4. THE ARCHIVIST — Ancient retainer of souls [Established direction]

An incomprehensibly old, conscious being with the innate power of Retention. For millennia before the kingdom, it elected unusually knowledgeable souls to archive as real, distinct, conscious individuals. Their memories, personalities, and capacity for Faith endure; their capacity for rebirth does not.

The Archivist's original collection contains knowledge from vanished civilizations. The Veil did not delete that knowledge; it imposed an artificial restriction upon new selections and the royal conduit. The Archivist can retain; that does not make it capable of restoring the retained.

5. THE CODEX — Manifestation of the Archivist [Established direction]

The Sword is the Archivist's physical manifestation and conduit, not an unrelated weapon with a prisoner inside. The mysterious old man encountered by the early king was another presentation of the same entity. The sword's appearance can age or be maintained without implying the Archivist's entire being is merely forged metal.

Function: Connect the mortal wielder to the accessible ancestral souls; channel Invocation (borrowed skills/knowledge) and provide a tangible weapon. It does not independently grant universal Dominion.

6. THE OATHBREAKER — Mortal royal descendant [Established direction]

A knight from a forgotten, illegitimate branch of the ancient royal line. His inherited Ancestral Dominion allows him to command eligible souls in the bloodline's authorized inheritance. Will enhances the efficiency of his personal authority; external Faith from rare genuine believers further amplifies him.

Key limitation: He cannot compel belief, access the Archivist's unrestricted ancient library, create souls, or restore souls retained in the sword.

PART IV — POWER SCALING TIERS

This is a narrative scale of supernatural scope, not a guaranteed one-on-one combat ranking. Faith and Will are above these tiers as operating principles, not fighters.

Tier Position Power and limitation
S — Ancient life authority The Queen Broadest established life/restoration authority: generates living forms, preserves viable souls, restores Champions. Above the Archivist in this chosen cosmological scale; not omnipotent.
A — Ancient continuity authority The Archivist Retains vast numbers of conscious souls and millennia of information; cannot restore them and remains restricted by the Veil's overlay.
B — Physical conduit The Codex An expression of the Archivist's power, not a second independent god. Access and combat output depend on the wielder's inheritance and control.
C → potentially much higher Oathbreaker Initially an inefficient, dangerous user of Dominion. Growth comes from mastery of Will, controlled invocation, and genuine Faith—not endlessly adding souls.
Unranked / variable Coalition / X Members possess soul manipulation and consumption, amplified by mass Faith. Their combined strength may be immense, but there is no settled direct tier relative to the Queen.

Character progression: Early: crude invocations, unstable control. Mid: deliberate skills, better selection and efficiency. Late: sophisticated combinations and stronger Will; massive potential, never unrestricted access to pre-Veil souls. The sword must remain reliably accessible through its signature slot rather than competing with normal card draw.

PART V — SOUL CONTINUITY: THE DEFINING OPERATIONS

Operation Primary user What happens to the soul? Identity survives? Can it be restored?
CONSUME Coalition / X Spiritual continuity is destroyed for sustenance and lifespan. No No
RETAIN Archivist Original conscious soul persists in an enduring archive, but loses the continuity needed for future embodiment. Yes No
PRESERVE Queen Original soul is held in a restorable condition; identity and vital continuity persist. Yes Yes
RESTORE Queen An already preserved soul returns to a living body. Yes Completed

Law of Spiritual Continuity: Only preserved souls can be restored. Retained ≠ preserved. A retained soul is a person, not a memory copy, but its lost restorable condition cannot simply be reconstructed by Faith or Will. No established method can turn an already-retained soul into a preserved soul.

Terminology note: The Queen may physically consume a person as part of her biological process, but she does not spiritually CONSUME their soul as X does. Her soul-operation is PRESERVE → RESTORE.

PART VI — CORE WORLD STORY (SEPARATE FROM THE OATHBREAKER)

Earth, Rifts, and the Accord [Established]

Modern Rifts appeared decades ago and connect other worlds through Earth, the shared crossroads. Earth became home to mixed populations, technologies, and beliefs. The Accord was founded to maintain coexistence; interworld tensions escalated into war. Heroes enter worlds to address crises, not simply seal every Rift.

Ancient exception [Working]: A rare, human-sized Rift linked the ancient kingdom with the Archivist centuries or millennia before the modern convergence. This precursor anomaly is distinct from the modern Earth-centered network.

Danger: A Rift is a pathway, not the origin of Faith. Some pathways expose ordinary worlds to extremely powerful entities capable of exploiting soul-based Faith, belief, and spiritual dependency.

X / the Coalition — The manufactured salvation [Established]

A small group of unusually powerful members of a founding world secretly manipulates Accord leaders. They orchestrate war, desperation, and promised rescue so populations choose to place Faith in them. Their abilities allow limited direct coercion of unwilling individuals, and much broader influence over genuine followers. Strong enough Faith can enable soul alteration and bodily transformation.

Faith sustains their strength; CONSUMPTION sustains their lifespan. Their own world's souls cannot replenish fast enough, so they expand their supply across worlds. Their seemingly perfect homeworld is built from the bodies of dead followers. They publicly condemn self-directed Will as dangerous or impure while cultivating dependence on their supposedly benevolent authority. Their individual identities and full goals remain open.

The Queen's colony and her game [Established direction]

A strange, primitive soulless insectoid colony is discovered through a Rift. The Coalition cannot ordinarily influence its subjects through Faith-based soul control. The Accord declares a truce to oppose the colony, allowing Champions from formerly hostile worlds to meet on that front.

The Queen chooses Champions opportunistically: potential usefulness and chance encounters, not moral worthiness, shape whom she preserves. She is neither an unquestioned savior nor necessarily a villain. Her ultimate objective is still hidden. A preserved Champion can oppose her, doubt her, or deny her Faith; preservation does not force genuine belief.

She senses the Coalition's disappearance of consumed souls [original lore]. Her colony changes through unlocks and development, reflecting the run-to-run progression hub. Exactly how her colony obtains technology and what price the Champions pay remain [Open].

Champions and rebirth loop [Established gameplay fiction]

Champions' linked souls let nearby allies recover from near death. Being downed weakens the link; when the whole party falls, the field connection fails and the Queen restores preserved Champions at her colony, ending the run. The exact proximity, solo exceptions, and consent/ownership rules remain [Open].

The ancient human kingdom and X's attack [Established direction]

Long before the modern Accord, an ancient human ruler encountered the old-man manifestation of the Archivist. The Archivist revealed Will—a truth already latent in souls—and the royal line developed Ancestral Dominion. Scholars studied Will and spiritual structures for generations.

When X attacked and exterminated the kingdom during the chaotic emergence of modern Rifts, scholars activated an unfinished emergency experiment. They retained the souls of their collapsing civilization within the Archivist, then hid the archive and its royal links through the Sovereign Veil. Some survivors of the royal lineage persisted outside the destroyed kingdom.

The kingdom's defeat is recent relative to its age: ancient founding and research can be centuries or millennia old, while the Coalition's destruction occurred only decades before game present.

PART VII — THE SOVEREIGN VEIL: ARTIFICIAL OVERLAY

What it is: An experimental Will-assisted spiritual construct engineered by dying scholars around the Archivist's existing Retention, not a new native power granted to him.

Layer Rule
Native Archivist May independently elect knowledgeable souls for retention.
Veil: future collection filter Future eligible transfers/selections are restricted to the designated royal bloodline.
Veil: inheritance access filter The Oathbreaker may invoke only his authorized bloodline inheritance; he cannot read or wield the Archivist's older, unrelated collection.
Veil: concealment Hides the royal spiritual link and ancestral archive from ordinary outside soul perception. Not general immunity.
Transfer curse Eligible descendants are routed into Retention at death unless an earlier, incompatible preservation condition takes precedence.

Important historical distinction: At the kingdom's destruction, the scholars mass-retained its dying citizens, including non-bloodline individuals, as part of the final emergency. Those souls remain real and archived. But the Oathbreaker's current Dominion is restricted to the bloodline's authorized inheritance. The wider kingdom and the Archivist's pre-kingdom souls are not automatically his to command or consult. This strict access rule supersedes an earlier proposed broader command over all kingdom citizens.

Design flaws: unproven, unfinished, no redundancy, no tested failover, no completed reverse spell. The Veil did not wound the Archivist or delete its history; it limited behavior through an artificial condition layered over its nature. It was built to save an inheritance, not tested for a clash with another ancient being.

Boundary: Better Will cannot simply overwrite the Veil. Nor can the Oathbreaker use Dominion to seize any stranger's soul. The Archivist's ancient knowledge still exists, but the conduit does not expose it to him.

PART VIII — THE OATHBREAKER: PERSONAL CHARACTER STORY

Identity and oath [Established direction]

The Oathbreaker is an experienced knight descended from a forgotten bastard branch of the ancient royal house. He swore to protect his home and people. He really is responsible for the catastrophic breach that killed those he loved, even if he never intended to forsake his oath. His journey is one of atonement, not a revelation that he was entirely innocent.

Visual identity: worn armor, clean polished helmet, maintained sword, golden eyes, white-grey short buzz cut, handsome mature face with no scar required. He can take off the helmet but never removes it where others can see him, because he is ashamed of the man beneath it.

The sword he carried [Working connective origin]

A plausible lineage: the dormant sword passed through his family's forgotten branch and was given to him by his father when he became a knight. He carried it for years as a seemingly ordinary family weapon. This route is not yet locked as canon; the sword's manifestation as the Archivist is the intended core relationship.

The Queen's condition — Why he escapes the archive [Established mechanics]

  1. Before his first death, the Queen elects him for preservation. She is pursuing her own agenda, not rewarding his virtue.
  2. At the moment of selection, her Preservation Bond conflicts with the descendant's pending Retention Transfer.
  3. That contradiction awakens the dormant Archivist. The Queen does not give the Oathbreaker Dominion: he already inherited its potential.
  4. The Archivist's response stirs the inherited connection; the Oathbreaker's Ancestral Dominion manifests, and the Sovereign Veil acts through its existing conditions.
  5. At his death, the Queen's already-active preservation takes priority over the death-triggered transfer curse. His soul is preserved, not retained; she can restore him.
  6. The curse still exists. If the Queen's preservation bond were absent at a future death, ancestral retention could reclaim him [Working consequence].

Why it works: The Queen acts earlier, not by rewriting Retention or defeating the Archivist. Two incompatible spiritual destinies cannot complete on the same soul. The Queen may not even detect the concealed archive; the Archivist senses an interference without automatically identifying her.

How they initially meet: [Open]—direct audience with the Queen, an encounter through her soulless subjects, or another colony contact. She must establish preservation while he is alive, before his fatal event.

The awakening, the breach, and the family [Established core; details Working]

The Queen's choice awakens the Archivist; the Archivist's response exposes the knight to dormant Ancestral Dominion. During a crisis defending his hometown, he attempts to wield power he barely understands. His own choice and uncontrolled invocation cause or materially contribute to the breach. Exact Rift/ritual mechanics remain [Open]—do not replace his responsibility with simple possession by the sword.

His parents, sisters, and blood-related townspeople die. Under the bloodline eligibility condition, their souls are retained by the Archivist—not consumed for fuel. They retain their identities and cannot be restored through the Queen's existing method. His own soul instead follows the Queen's Preservation Bond.

The silent family and genuine Faith [Established direction]

Unlike many resentful bloodline ancestors, his immediate family continues to believe in him. They do not speak. Instead, they quietly polish his helmet, sharpen and clean his sword, and tend his gear. This supplies the lore behind the cleaner blade tip and helmet against the worn armor. Their silence does not prove forgiveness or innocence; their care expresses real Faith he cannot command into existence.

A very small number of living people whom he saved in early knighthood and later travels also genuinely believe in him. Most other souls serve under Dominion, not loyalty. Faith from these few people amplifies his soul; Will is his unstable inward belief. These are not different energy types.

Signature weapon and game identity [Established design direction]

The Codex occupies a dedicated signature-weapon slot, always reliably available regardless of normal draw pile/deck bloat. Cards invoke eligible ancestral techniques, movement, defense, and attacks on the hex board. Growth is through refined Will, better invocation efficiency, positioning, and meaningful soul interactions—not simply accumulating unlimited new souls or arbitrary damage multipliers.

Character thesis: He can command countless inherited voices, yet cannot compel the belief of even one. The dead who know him best say nothing, but care for him anyway.

PART IX — CHRONOLOGY AT A GLANCE

Era Event
Distant past The Archivist collects knowledgeable souls from many civilizations; its full age is unknown.
Ancient kingdom's beginnings The Archivist appears as an old man to a ruler; the king learns of Will; Ancestral Dominion and spiritual study develop.
Generations of research Scholars explore the soul, Will efficiency, royal inheritance, and contingency mechanisms.
Decades before the game Modern Earth-centered Rifts appear; X attacks the kingdom amid the chaos.
Kingdom's final hours Scholars mass-retain dying souls, activate the unfinished Veil, restrict future collection and access, and die without finishing a reversal.
Later The royal bloodline survives in scattered, forgotten branches; the sword remains dormant.
Oathbreaker's knighthood He earns limited living Faith, serves his people, and carries the sword (family inheritance route Working).
Queen's choice The Preservation Bond creates an anomalous conflict; Archivist awakens; dormant Dominion stirs.
The breach The knight misuses newly accessible power while defending his home; his family dies and is retained. He becomes the Oathbreaker.
Game present He pursues atonement under the Queen's rebirth condition, the Veil's restrictions, and the family's quiet Faith.

PART X — NON-NEGOTIABLE LORE RULES

  • Faith is primary; Will is inward Faith. Neither is a free-form spell system.
  • Faith empowers, but does not manufacture innate talents. Genuine belief cannot be forced.
  • The Queen is above the Archivist in the chosen hierarchy, but neither is outside Faith.
  • The Queen creates/restores living bodies, not proven souls from nothing.
  • Consume destroys; Retain endures without rebirth; Preserve keeps rebirth possible. Only the preserved can be restored.
  • The sword is the Archivist's manifestation, and the old man is the same entity.
  • The Veil is an untested overlay, not an innate cosmic law; it did not wound the Archivist.
  • Oathbreaker has bloodline-gated access only, not free access to the Archivist's millennia-old unrelated collection.
  • The Queen's selection awakens the Archivist, not the Oathbreaker's hereditary ability from nothing.
  • Oathbreaker caused the breach; his family genuinely believes in him but never speaks; he chooses to hide his face.
  • X is the Coalition, not the Accord. Modern Rifts converge on Earth, and the Rifts themselves are not Faith's origin.
